<div class="cgdoc" markdown="1">

On September 10, 2026, the Census Bureau proposed changing who gets counted for
congressional apportionment. Under proposed section 60.4, foreign citizens are counted
only if they are "either citizens or lawful permanent residents of the United States."
Everyone else — people on student and work visas, people awaiting asylum decisions,
people here illegally — would be, in the rule's words, "[n]ot counted for
apportionment."

The comment period, first set to close October 13, has been extended to
**November 2, 2026**.[^fr]

## Who stops being counted

The rule draws its line at lawful permanent residence, not citizenship. Citizens count.
Green-card holders count. Everyone else living here does not.

That removes about 11 million people from the apportionment base — out of roughly 23
million non-citizens in the country. Close to half the non-citizen population goes on
being counted, which is why the effect below is about half the size of what a
citizen-only count would produce.

<!-- population totals: generated by build_poptable.js, do not edit -->
<dl class="pt-stats">
  <div><dt>projected population in 2030, fifty states</dt><dd>352.4 million</dd></div>
  <div><dt>not counted under the rule (3.2%)</dt><dd>11.4 million</dd></div>
  <div><dt>counted for apportionment</dt><dd>340.9 million</dd></div>
  <div><dt>people per seat, down from 810,000</dt><dd>784,000</dd></div>
</dl>
<!-- end population totals -->

Where those 11 million live is what decides the rest, and they are nowhere near evenly
spread. The rule takes 5.9% of Texas's projected population and 4.7% of California's,
against 1.5% of Wisconsin's and 1.3% of Michigan's — roughly four times as much,
proportionally, from the first pair as the second. Apportionment divides a fixed 435
seats by relative population, so a state loses ground whenever it is cut harder than
the rest. No state has to lose a single resident for its delegation to shrink.

## Three seats move

| State | 2020 | 2030, current rules | 2030, proposed rule | Change from the rule |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| California | 52 | 48 (−4) | 47 | −1 |
| Texas | 38 | 42 (+4) | 41 | −1 |
| Illinois | 17 | 16 (−1) | 15 | −1 |
| Michigan | 13 | 12 (−1) | 13 | +1 |
| Tennessee | 9 | 9 (—) | 10 | +1 |
| Wisconsin | 8 | 7 (−1) | 8 | +1 |
| Florida | 28 | 31 (+3) | 31 | — |
| New York | 26 | 25 (−1) | 25 | — |
| Pennsylvania | 17 | 16 (−1) | 16 | — |
| Georgia | 14 | 15 (+1) | 15 | — |
| North Carolina | 14 | 15 (+1) | 15 | — |
| Arizona | 9 | 10 (+1) | 10 | — |
| Minnesota | 8 | 7 (−1) | 7 | — |
| Oregon | 6 | 5 (−1) | 5 | — |
| Utah | 4 | 5 (+1) | 5 | — |
| Idaho | 2 | 3 (+1) | 3 | — |
| Rhode Island | 2 | 1 (−1) | 1 | — |

The middle column is the story most people already know: on current trends, twelve
seats move between sixteen states between 2020 and 2030, with Texas up four and
Florida up three. The right-hand column is what the proposed rule would do *on top of
that* — and it is a much smaller change. Three seats, six states.

## Texas loses

The obvious reading of this rule is that it helps Republicans. The arithmetic is not
that clean.

Texas is projected to gain four seats by 2030 under current rules — the largest gain in
the country. Under the proposed rule it gains three. Texas grew the way it did partly
through immigration, and a rule that stops counting part of that growth takes back some
of what it gave.

The seats do land in states Trump carried in 2024 — Michigan, Tennessee, and Wisconsin
— so on the 2024 map the net is two seats toward Trump-won states and two away from
Harris-won ones. That overstates it. Michigan and Wisconsin are the closest states in
presidential politics: Biden carried both in 2020 and Trump both in 2024, each time by
less than three points, and both have Democratic governors. Only Tennessee's new seat
lands in a reliably Republican state.

Who draws the lines after 2030 cuts the same way. Republicans would draw one more
district in Tennessee and one fewer in Texas, which nets to zero; Democrats in Illinois
would draw one fewer. California's and Michigan's districts are drawn by independent
citizens' commissions — California's temporary legislative map runs only through
2030 — and Wisconsin's needs the governor's signature as well as the legislature's.
Counting only districts one party draws alone, Democrats lose one and Republicans come
out even. For Republicans, that is a thin return on a change this disruptive.

## Inside the states

Apportionment decides how many seats a state gets. Districting decides where the lines
fall, and there the rule bites differently — and more widely, because it reaches every
state with more than one district, not just the six whose seat counts change.
Districts must hold equal population, so a district that loses part of its count has
to reach further out to get back to the line, absorbing neighboring territory and,
with it, neighboring voters.

Running the districts actually in force for the 2026 election — the ten states that
redrew mid-decade included[^plans] — against a citizens-and-green-card-holders base:

| District | Non-citizen share | Shortfall | vs. ideal | Presidential lean |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Texas 33 | 24% | −68,600 | −9.6% | D +33 |
| New Jersey 8 | 27% | −69,100 | −9.4% | D +45 \* |
| Texas 28 | 20% | −58,900 | −8.2% | R +10 |
| California 34 | 24% | −54,300 | −7.5% | D +53 |
| Texas 7 | 23% | −50,300 | −7.0% | D +24 |

<small>Lean is the two-party margin in the 2024 presidential race, except
<span markdown="1">\*</span> New Jersey, where 2020 is the most recent result
published at block level.[^dra]</small>

Across all 429 districts in multi-district states the tilt is consistent: Democratic-
leaning districts sit 0.7% below their state's new ideal on average, Republican-leaning
ones 0.6% above. Of the fifty most underpopulated districts, thirty-eight lean
Democratic. Map-drawers in most states would be moving people out of Republican
districts and into Democratic ones — the raw material for a favorable redraw.

Not all of the hardest-hit districts lean Democratic. Three of the ten voted for
Trump: Texas's 28th and 15th, along the border, and Florida's 27th in Miami. They are
heavily Hispanic districts that moved toward Trump in 2024, and they are part of why
the partisan effect below is smaller than the raw pattern suggests.

### Wherever the lines are drawn

Those are today's districts. The maps drawn after 2030 will have different lines, so
it is worth asking the question without any map at all: within each state, how much
of the population lives in places that vote Democratic, counting everyone, and how
much counting only the people the rule keeps? Using each census tract's estimated
exclusion (mapped under "Where the people who keep counting live") and its most
recent presidential result:[^dra]

| State | Seats under the rule | In Harris-won tracts, everyone | Counted only | Shift, in districts |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Texas | 41 | 45.1% | 44.0% | −0.45 |
| California | 47 | 76.7% | 76.2% | −0.25 |
| Florida | 31 | 33.7% | 33.1% | −0.21 |
| New Jersey \* | 12 | 71.2% | 70.1% | −0.13 |
| Illinois | 15 | 67.4% | 66.7% | −0.11 |
| North Carolina | 15 | 48.1% | 47.5% | −0.09 |
| Arizona | 10 | 46.7% | 45.8% | −0.09 |
| Georgia | 15 | 50.3% | 49.8% | −0.08 |

<small>The shift is the change in share times the state's seats: the number of
districts' worth of population that moves out of Harris-won tracts.
<span markdown="1">\*</span> New Jersey is measured on 2020, so its tracts are
Biden-won.</small>

Every one of the 42 states with more than one district moves the same way. Added
up, about 2.3 districts' worth of population leaves Democratic-voting neighborhoods
nationwide. Many of the hardest-hit neighborhoods are only narrowly Democratic,
though, so weighting each person by how their tract actually voted cuts the net to
about 0.9 of a district.

This is suggestive, not a forecast. It says which way equal-population lines would
have to move, not who will draw them or how: a map-drawer can concentrate the change
in one district or spread it thin, and in a close seat a few points of new territory
can decide the outcome. But the direction holds in every state, and it falls hardest
in the same places the apportionment does — Texas and California above all.

## State by state

Six states change hands, and they divide into two kinds. Three are cut deeply
enough to lose a seat. Three are cut so lightly that they gain one without
anything happening to them at all.

### {% include state-mark.html st="TX" %}Texas gains three seats instead of four

Texas has the largest projected gain in the country — four seats, on the strength
of a decade of growth. The rule removes 5.9% of its projected population, a bigger
share than any other state, and takes one of those four seats back. Much of what
Texas grew by is what the rule stops counting.

### {% include state-mark.html st="CA" %}California loses a fifth seat

California is already projected to fall from 52 seats to 48 on population trend
alone. The rule removes 4.7% of its count and takes a fifth. It sits about 228,000
people short of holding that seat — the largest single loss on the board, and the
one least sensitive to how the rule is read: California loses at least one seat
under every version of it.

### {% include state-mark.html st="IL" %}Illinois loses a second seat

Illinois drops from 17 to 16 on trend, and the rule takes it to 15. It misses
keeping that sixteenth seat by about 48,000 people, one of the closest calls in the
country: a modestly smaller estimate of its excluded population would be enough to
save it.

### {% include state-mark.html st="MI" %}Michigan keeps the seat it was going to lose

Michigan is projected to fall from 13 seats to 12. Under the rule it stays at 13 —
not because anything changed in Michigan, but because 1.3% of its population is
excluded against 4.7% in California and 5.9% in Texas. Nothing had to happen here
for the delegation to survive.

### {% include state-mark.html st="WI" %}Wisconsin also keeps one it was losing

The same story as Michigan, and one of the narrowest margins in the set. Wisconsin
goes from 8 seats to 7 on trend and back to 8 under the rule, holding that eighth
seat by roughly 51,000 people. It is among the first seats that would move if the
excluded population were estimated slightly differently.

### {% include state-mark.html st="TN" %}Tennessee gains outright

Tennessee holds at 9 seats under current projections and reaches 10 under the
rule. It is the only state in the set that gains a seat it was not otherwise in
line for. Its 10th is held by about 61,000 people.

<p class="pt-note">Every figure is a 2030 projection, not a count. State totals are
projected from Census Bureau vintage 2025 estimates. Non-citizens are projected from the
ACS one-year estimates for 2005 through 2024, each state on its own weighted trend (see
Method). Green-card holders are each state's projected share of its non-citizens, carried
forward on the same kind of trend from DHS's annual estimates for 2015 through 2024; the
remaining non-citizens are not counted. The District of Columbia is not apportioned and
is left out.</p>
<p class="pt-note"><span class="pt-mark">†</span> DHS publishes green-card estimates for
the twenty largest states only. For the rest, its pooled total is divided in proportion
to each state's non-citizen population, so the split in these states is an assumption,
not a state figure.</p>
</details>
<!-- end population table -->

## Where the people who keep counting live

Half the non-citizen population goes on being counted, so where *that* half lives
matters as much as where non-citizens live — and the two maps are not the same.

DHS publishes green-card counts by county for the fifty largest metro areas, but
only for people who became green-card holders in the past ten years.[^dhs-county]
That is not a count of everyone who holds one, so it is used here only to divide
each state's total among its metro counties. Set against the ACS non-citizen count
for the same counties,[^acs-county] the share still counted runs from 20% to 82%:

| Metro area | Non-citizens | Still counted | Share |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Sacramento | 186,000 | 153,000 | 82% |
| New York | 2,409,000 | 1,802,000 | 75% |
| San Francisco | 619,000 | 454,000 | 73% |
| Miami | 1,022,000 | 554,000 | 54% |
| Los Angeles | 1,885,000 | 986,000 | 52% |
| Chicago | 791,000 | 407,000 | 52% |
| Houston | 978,000 | 415,000 | 42% |
| Dallas–Fort Worth | 878,000 | 355,000 | 40% |
| Bakersfield | 107,000 | 35,000 | 33% |
| McAllen | 157,000 | 32,000 | 20% |

The widest gaps are inside states, not between them. California's metros all
draw on the same statewide total, yet Sacramento keeps 82% of its non-citizens in
the count and Bakersfield 33%; Los Angeles, at 52%, would stop counting about
900,000 people. In Texas, Houston and Dallas keep about two in five and McAllen
one in five. A count of non-citizens alone would treat these places alike. Under
this rule they are not.

Two caveats matter for reading these, and they pull in opposite directions. DHS
records a green-card holder at the place they *obtained* that status, not where
they live now, so long-settled gateways — New York above all — carry people who
left long ago, which inflates the share still counted. And because the county
counts cover only the last ten years of admissions, those same gateways, whose
green-card holders mostly arrived earlier, get less than their share when a state's
total is divided, which deflates it. The size of neither error is known.

Mapped, the difference is stark. This is not a shaded-county map: each tract
contributes its own value[^tracts] and the space between is a distance-weighted blend,
contoured like a weather chart. It shows the share of **everyone** — not of
non-citizens — who would stop being counted, because that is the quantity that
moves representation.

  <line class="xs-rule" x1="0" y1="563" x2="960" y2="563"/>
  <text class="xs-cap" x="24" y="580">Share of all residents who would stop being counted</text>
  <rect class="xs-b0" x="24" y="590" width="64" height="11"/>
  <text class="xs-tick" x="24" y="614">0%</text>
  <rect class="xs-b1" x="88" y="590" width="64" height="11"/>
  <text class="xs-tick" x="88" y="614">2%</text>
  <rect class="xs-b2" x="152" y="590" width="64" height="11"/>
  <text class="xs-tick" x="152" y="614">5%</text>
  <rect class="xs-b3" x="216" y="590" width="64" height="11"/>
  <text class="xs-tick" x="216" y="614">10%</text>
  <rect class="xs-b4" x="280" y="590" width="64" height="11"/>
  <text class="xs-tick" x="280" y="614">15%</text>
  <text class="xs-tick" x="344" y="614" text-anchor="middle">25%+</text>
  <rect class="xs-b1" x="422" y="590" width="26" height="11"/>
  <rect x="422" y="590" width="26" height="11" fill="url(#xs-hatch)"/>
  <text class="xs-cap" x="456" y="599">no county count: assumes the state’s green-card share</text>
</svg>
</div>
<!-- end exclusion surface -->

The country is mostly pale. The rule reaches 10% or more of the population in a
handful of places: the Rio Grande Valley, Dallas and Houston, the Central Valley,
northern New Jersey across the Hudson from Manhattan, and the meatpacking towns of
the High Plains around the Oklahoma Panhandle. Los Angeles, Miami, Orlando and
Atlanta sit in the band just below. Half of all tracts sit below 1.5%.

Two things about how to read it. The surface stops where its subject does — where
almost nobody lives, nothing is drawn, which is why the interior is full of holes.

The hatching marks where the map is less certain. Every tract's figure comes from the
same calculation: its non-citizens, minus the share of them estimated to hold green
cards. What differs is where that green-card share comes from. DHS counts recent
green-card holders county by county only in the fifty largest metro areas, and
there each county gets its part of the state's total in proportion to that count,
as described above. Everywhere else, the hatched areas, there is no county count,
so each county is assumed to have the same green-card share as its state as a
whole, and only the split among its tracts is estimated.

That assumption is not harmless. In the counties where both figures exist, using the
state share instead of the county count changes a tract's value by a little under
one percentage point on average — enough to move **18% of tracts into a different
color band** on this scale. About a quarter of the non-citizen population lives in
hatched areas.

## How solid is this

Reasonable people will estimate the excluded population differently, so it is worth
asking how much the answer depends on that choice. Bounding it from both sides:

| Scenario | Excluded population | Seats moved |
|---|---:|---:|
| Unauthorized immigrants only | 12.7 million | 3 |
| **Citizens and green-card holders counted (the rule)** | **11.4 million** | **3** |
| Citizens only | 23.4 million | 6 |

The first two rows come from genuinely independent sources — one from DHS estimates of
the lawful permanent resident population, the other from Pew's estimates of the
unauthorized population — and they land within 1.4 million of each other and give the
same answer. The third row is the citizen-only count that is *not* what was proposed;
it is here as the ceiling, and note that even at that ceiling only six seats move.

Across all three scenarios the same states move the same way: Illinois and Texas lose,
Michigan, Tennessee, and Wisconsin gain. That much is solid.

How solid is a harder question than it looks, because the last few seats are always
decided by very little. A seat is worth about 784,000 people in 2030. Under the
proposed rule, Wisconsin holds its eighth seat by roughly 51,000 of them and Tennessee
its tenth by 61,000 — under a tenth of a seat either way. Illinois, one of the three
states that loses, misses keeping its sixteenth by about 48,000.

And the closest call on the whole map belongs to a state that does not appear in the
table at all: South Carolina finishes some 22,000 people short of an eighth seat,
less than half of one percent of its population. Any of these could land the other way on a
different set of assumptions, or on the actual count.

South Carolina's is not a hypothetical. The largest assumption behind the rule's
column is how the non-citizen population is carried forward to 2030. The ACS count
rose from 21.2 million in 2021 to 24.3 million in 2024, and the trend used here treats
most of that rise as temporary, projecting 23.4 million for 2030. Weight the last few
years heavily enough that the rise keeps going, and a fourth seat moves: Florida loses
one and South Carolina gains its eighth. The other six states move exactly as above.
Which is closer to right depends on whether the 2022–24 increase lasts, and no trend
line can say. The green-card share matters less: holding each state's share at its
2024 level, rather than projecting it, moves the same three seats.

## What this does not capture

Two other parts of the proposal are harder to model and could matter as much as the
population base. Proposed section 60.2 would require that a person's residence be
where they "lawfully spent the greatest number of days," evidenced by tax records, and
would compress the enumeration period to January 3 through April 1. Both narrow the
count further in ways no population estimate here accounts for.

Nor does any of this address the constitutional question. The Fourteenth Amendment
directs that representatives be apportioned "counting the whole number of persons in
each State." In *Department of Commerce v. New York* (2019) the Court rejected the
last administration's stated rationale for a citizenship question as "contrived," and
in *Evenwel v. Abbott* (2016) it upheld total population as a districting base without
requiring it. Whether "persons" can be read to exclude lawfully present people on
visas is a question for the courts, and it will get there.

## Method

Apportionment is calculated by the method of equal proportions (Huntington–Hill), 435
seats, one to each state first — the method in use since 1941.

State population comes from Census Bureau vintage 2025 estimates, projected to
2030.[^census] Who makes up that population is projected separately. Each state's
total, citizen, and non-citizen counts come from the American Community Survey's
one-year estimates for 2005 through 2024,[^acs] and each series is carried forward to
2030 on its own trend, so the non-citizen population in these figures is a 2030
projection, not a 2024 count. Lawful permanent residents come from the Department of
Homeland Security's annual estimates for January 1 of each year from 2015 through
2024.[^dhs-state] Each year's figure is set against that year's ACS non-citizen count,
and each state's green-card share of its non-citizens is carried forward to 2030 on
its own trend, then applied to the projected non-citizen population. Nationally that
share rose from 58% in 2015 to 62% in 2019–21 and has fallen since, to 52% in 2024, as
the non-citizen population grew faster than the green-card population; the projection
puts it at 51% in 2030. DHS names only the twenty or so largest states each year, so
the rest carry a common share of its pooled total. The non-citizens left over are the
people the rule stops counting,
and their share of each state's projected ACS population is applied to the Bureau's
2030 total. Unauthorized estimates from the Pew Research Center[^pew] are used only to
bound the result. Each source is used for what it measures well: the Bureau's
estimates for how many people, the survey for who they are.

Every projection here is made the same way. It is not one year's change carried
forward, or an average of recent changes: a straight line is fitted through every year
of the series by weighted least squares and extended to 2030, with each year weighted
by e<sup>−0.15 × (years before the latest)</sup>. For the ACS series that means 2024
counts in full, 2023 at 86%, 2021 at 64%, 2019 at 47%, 2010 at 12%, and 2005 at 6%; the
four years since the pandemic carry about half the total weight, and 2005 through 2014
about a fifth. There is no 2020 one-year ACS file, so that year is simply absent. The
Bureau's state totals are fitted the same way over 2020 through 2025, and the
green-card share over 2015 through 2024.

Recent years get more weight because the 2020–21 population base is distorted by the
pandemic and tells us less about 2030 than 2023–24 does. That choice matters, and it
is why the middle column here moves twelve seats
where the [2020-to-2030 map]({{ '/maps/apportionment-2020-2030/' | relative_url }}) on
this site moves thirteen: that map is fitted without the weighting. For that column the
*rate* of down-weighting changes nothing at all — every decay constant from 0.15 to 1.0
moves twelve seats.

The rule's column is less settled, because the non-citizen series is not a straight
line. Fitted across all nineteen years of data at the decay used here, 0.15, the jump
of the last three years mostly washes out, and the projection for 2030 comes in below
the 2024 count. Any decay constant up to 0.3 moves the same three seats. From 0.34
up, the recent years dominate: the non-citizen projection climbs past 24.5 million,
the green-card share keeps falling, and a fourth seat moves, as set out under "How
solid is this."

The district figures are present-day, not projected: 2020 census population by block
with citizenship from the ACS five-year estimates,[^dra] joined to the block assignment
file for the plan each state will actually use in 2026.[^plans] Blocks nest inside
districts exactly, so nothing is interpolated — every state's districts sum to its
census population to the person. Two assumptions remain. Block data carries citizenship
only for the voting-age population, so each district's non-citizen count is scaled to
all ages by its state's ratio from the ACS. And the share of those non-citizens who
hold green cards comes from the state estimate, divided among a state's big-metro
counties in proportion to DHS's county counts of recent green-card holders where
those exist,[^dhs-county] and weighted by how much of the district sits in each
county.

A projection is not a census. Small differences in state population can decide the last
few seats, and the 2020 census itself [missed people in fourteen
states](https://www.census.gov/library/stories/2022/05/2020-census-undercount-overcount-rates-by-state.html).
The direction and rough size of these shifts are more trustworthy than any single
state's seat count.

Code and data: [github.com/jcervas](https://github.com/jcervas).

[^fr]: Bureau of the Census, *Decennial Census of the Population of Americans; Proposed
    Residence Criteria and Proposed Regulations for Demographic Questions*, 91 Fed. Reg.
    57524 (Sept. 10, 2026) (to be codified at 15 C.F.R. pts. 60, 70), RIN 0607-AA75,
    docket USBC-2026-0628.
    [federalregister.gov](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/09/10/2026-18481/decennial-census-of-the-population-of-americans-proposed-residence-criteria-and-proposed-regulations).
    The comment period was extended from October 13 to November 2, 2026, by 91 Fed. Reg.
    63207 (Oct. 5, 2026).
    [federalregister.gov](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/05/2026-20328/decennial-census-of-the-population-of-americans-proposed-residence-criteria-and-proposed-regulations)

[^census]: U.S. Census Bureau, Vintage 2025 state population estimates, 2020–2025
    ([NST-EST2025-ALLDATA.csv](https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2025/state/totals/NST-EST2025-ALLDATA.csv);
    [table page](https://www.census.gov/data/tables/time-series/demo/popest/2020s-state-total.html)),
    projected to 2030 by weighted linear extrapolation. The 2020 seats are the
    [2020 Census apportionment](https://www.census.gov/data/tables/2020/dec/2020-apportionment-data.html).

[^acs]: U.S. Census Bureau, American Community Survey one-year estimates, table B05001
    (citizenship status by nativity), 2005–2024, by state; total population is
    B05001_001E and non-citizens B05001_006E. No 2020 one-year file was released.
    Each year is available from the Census API, for example
    [2024](https://api.census.gov/data/2024/acs/acs1?get=NAME,B05001_001E,B05001_006E&for=state:*)
    (change the year in the address for the others), or as
    [table B05001 on data.census.gov](https://data.census.gov/table/ACSDT1Y2024.B05001).

[^acs-county]: U.S. Census Bureau, American Community Survey 2023 one-year estimates,
    table B05001, by county
    ([Census API](https://api.census.gov/data/2023/acs/acs1?get=NAME,B05001_001E,B05001_006E&for=county:*)).
    One-year figures are published only for counties of 65,000 or more.

[^tracts]: Tract estimates combine the American Community Survey 2019–2023 five-year
    estimates, tables B01003 (total population), B05005 (period of entry by
    citizenship), and B06009 (place of birth by educational attainment), at tract and
    county level, from the
    [ACS summary file](https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/acs/summary_file/2023/table-based-SF/data/5YRData/),
    with each state's green-card total from DHS divided among its counties as
    described in the text (see the DHS notes). Boundaries are from
    [us-atlas](https://github.com/topojson/us-atlas), built from the Census Bureau's
    cartographic boundary files.

[^dhs-state]: Office of Homeland Security Statistics, *Estimates of the Lawful Permanent
    Resident Population in the United States*, table of all LPRs (not only those
    eligible to naturalize) by initial or current state of residence. Each year is
    taken from the latest edition that reports it:
    2015–2018 from the [2015–2019 edition (PDF)](https://ohss.dhs.gov/sites/default/files/2023-12/lpr_population_estimates_2015_-_2019.pdf.pdf), tbl. A3;
    2019–2021 from the [2019–2021 edition (PDF)](https://ohss.dhs.gov/sites/default/files/2023-12/22_0405_plcy_lpr_population_estimates_2019_-_2021.pdf), tbl. A3;
    2022 from the [2022 edition (PDF)](https://ohss.dhs.gov/sites/default/files/2023-12/2022_0920_plcy_lawful_permenent_resident_population_estimate_2022_0.pdf), tbl. A3;
    2023 from the revised figures in the [2024 and Revised 2023 edition (PDF)](https://ohss.dhs.gov/sites/default/files/2024-11/2024_1108_ohss_lawful_permenent_resident_population_estimate_2024_and_revised_2023.pdf), tbl. 3,
    which supersede the [2023 edition (PDF)](https://ohss.dhs.gov/sites/default/files/2023-12/2023_1017_plcy_lawful_permenent_resident_population_estimate_2023.pdf);
    and 2024 from the revised figures in the
    [2025 and Revised 2024 edition](https://ohss.dhs.gov/topics/immigration/lawful-permanent-residents/population-estimates/fy-25-lpr-pop-estimates)
    (June 2026), tbl. 6. That edition's January 2025 figure is not used, because no ACS
    year yet pairs with it. Only the twenty or so largest states are named in any year;
    the remainder are published as a single "Other" total, with a small "Unknown"
    residual, and both are distributed here in proportion to ACS non-citizen
    population. All editions are listed on the
    [historical estimates page](https://ohss.dhs.gov/topics/immigration/lawful-permanent-residents/population-estimates/historical).

[^dhs-county]: Office of Homeland Security Statistics, *Estimated LPR Population and
    Number Eligible to Naturalize Among Recent LPR Admissions by County, Top 50 Core
    Based Statistical Areas: January 1, 2025*. Counts only people granted LPR status in
    the ten years before the estimate date, about 7.4 million in 335 counties, so it is
    not a count of all green-card holders. It is used here only to divide each state's
    total among its covered counties. Residence is where LPR status was obtained, not
    current residence. Two rows are re-matched to the ACS by hand: Baltimore City and
    Fairfax City had been joined to the counties of the same name.
    [Spreadsheet (XLSX)](https://ohss.dhs.gov/system/files/2026-07/2026_0604_ohss_lpr-and-eligible-to-naturalize-jan-01-2025%20v2.xlsx);
    [all county files](https://ohss.dhs.gov/topics/immigration/lawful-permanent-residents/population-estimates/by-county-top-cbsas).

[^pew]: Pew Research Center, *U.S. Unauthorized Immigrant Population Reached a Record 14
    Million in 2023* (Aug. 21, 2025), detailed tables by state, 1990–2023. Estimates are
    adjusted for survey undercount and so are not on the same footing as the ACS counts;
    they are used here only to bound the result.
    [pewresearch.org](https://www.pewresearch.org/race-and-ethnicity/2025/08/21/u-s-unauthorized-immigrant-population-reached-a-record-14-million-in-2023/)

[^dra]: Dave's Redistricting App, block-level demographic and election files (v06, June
    2025): 2020 census population and voting-age population, citizen voting-age
    population from the ACS five-year estimates, and precinct results disaggregated to
    blocks. Fourteen states have no 2024 presidential file; those districts are labeled
    from 2020. The block files are published per state as
    `Demographic_Data_Block_XX.v06.zip` (XX is the state abbreviation) at
    `https://data.dra2020.net/file/dra-block-data/`; see
    [davesredistricting.org](https://davesredistricting.org).

[^plans]: Congressional plans as of the 2026 election, from Dave's Redistricting App's
    official maps: 2026 plans for Alabama, California, Florida, Louisiana, North
    Carolina, Ohio, Tennessee, Texas and Utah, and 2022 or 2024 plans elsewhere.
    Missouri's 2026 map was blocked in court, so Missouri is counted on its 2022 lines;
    enactment status follows the table behind this site's
    [mid-decade redistricting map]({{ '/maps/mid-decade-redistricting/' | relative_url }}).
